I wish I could give you a straight answer, but I just really don't know.\n\nDoctor: Have these concerns about your face ever caused you to avoid certain situations, like public events or even work-related tasks, because you were afraid of how your face might look or how others might react to it?\n\nPatient: Oh my gosh, yes. It’s... honestly, it’s getting to the point where it’s really messing with my actual job, which is terrifying because I love what I do and I'm only twenty, so I really need to prove I can handle this industry. But, like, just a couple of weeks ago, I actually missed part of a major planning deadline, and it was entirely because of this. We were putting together these digital welcome packets and event materials for a huge corporate retreat—like, a three-day thing with team-building exercises and gala dinners, the whole nine yards. And part of the packet was this \"Meet Your Event Team\" page, which meant I had to submit a headshot. \n\nI mean, I completely spiraled. I must have spent hours—literally hours—just trying to get a picture where the unevenness in my face didn't look completely glaring. I was taking photos in my bathroom, then by the window, then in the hallway, trying to find lighting that would just hide it. And then I kept putting off sending the file to the graphic designer because I just couldn't bear the thought of all those corporate executives staring at my asymmetrical face in their welcome brochures. I kept thinking, \"I can't send this, they'll think I look awful.\" So I just kept delaying it, and tweaking it, and obsessing over it, to the point where I completely missed the deadline to get the materials to the printer on time. My boss had to rush-order it, and I had to make up some excuse about my laptop freezing because I was too embarrassed to say, \"Hey, I couldn't send the email because I spent four hours agonizing over my face.\"\n\nAnd it's not just the big stuff like that, either. It’s bleeding into the everyday things. Like, vendor calls and meeting prep take me so, so much longer than they should now. It used to be that if I had a Zoom call with a florist or a rental company to go over a floor plan, I'd just open my laptop and join the meeting. Now? I need at least thirty to forty-five minutes of prep time just for a basic check-in call. I have to set up my ring light exactly right, I have to position my camera at this very specific high angle, and I spend forever adjusting my hair to try and cover the side of my face that looks worse. If the lighting changes because a cloud goes over the sun outside, I panic and have to turn my whole desk around. \n\nSo, yeah, I try to avoid having my camera on whenever I can get away with it, but in this business, people want to see your face, they want that personal connection. So when I have to do it, the amount of time I waste just trying to make sure I don't look horrifying is... it's exhausting. It just drags everything out, and I'm constantly working late now just to catch up on the actual planning work I should have been doing while I was busy fussing over my camera angle. So, yes, it definitely makes me avoid certain things, and when I can't avoid them, it just takes over completely.\n\nDoctor: Have these concerns about your face ever affected areas of your life outside of work, like your personal relationships, hobbies, or social activities?\n\nPatient: Um... honestly? Not really? Or, I mean, I guess I'm not entirely sure because... well, my whole life right now is basically just work. \n\nLike, I'm only twenty, and getting into event planning right out of high school means I have to hustle twice as hard as everyone else who actually went to college for hospitality or communications or whatever. So, as far as hobbies or social activities go... I don't really have any right now. My typical week is like, sixty to seventy hours of vendor meetings, site visits, drafting proposals, and answering client emails at two in the morning. I don't really go out with friends much anymore because by the time the weekend rolls around, I'm usually working a wedding or running a corporate retreat, or if I actually have a day off, I'm just completely dead on my couch catching up on sleep.\n\nSo I haven't really had the chance to see if this whole face thing ruins my personal life, because I kind of just... don't have one to ruin? Like, I'm not dating anyone right now, so I don't have to worry about a boyfriend looking at my face in weird lighting or waking up next to someone and panicking about the angle they're seeing me from. And my friends from high school, we mostly just text or send each other TikToks in our group chat, so I don't have to sit across from them at a restaurant and wonder if they're staring at my asymmetry over brunch. \n\nI mean, I think about it sometimes. Like, maybe if I did have time to go to parties or join a gym or, I don't know, take a pottery class or something normal people do, I'd probably be in the bathroom there checking my camera too. I can totally picture myself doing that. But since my entire existence is just this job, all that panic and obsession is just totally concentrated on my work stuff. Which is almost worse, honestly, when you think about it, because work is the one thing I really, really can't mess up. If I screw up a hobby, who cares, right? Like, oh well, I'm bad at pottery. But if I screw up a client's 50th-anniversary gala because I was hiding behind a curtain analyzing my chin on my iPhone, that's my whole career on the line. \n\nSo... yeah. I guess outside of work, it's not really a thing I've noticed causing problems. But only because work is literally my entire universe right now, so there's nowhere else for it to even show up, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Even though you're very focused on work, have you ever felt so self-conscious about your face that you worried it would affect your ability to connect with someone new, like a potential friend or romantic partner, if you had the chance?\n\nPatient: Um... I don't think so? Like, honestly, no, I haven't really had that specific fear, to be completely honest. Because, well, to even worry about whether my face is going to ruin a connection with a potential friend or a romantic partner, I feel like I'd have to actually be in a position where I'm trying to meet people like that, right? And I'm just... not. \n\nI mean, sure, I meet new people literally every single day, but they're always, you know, caterers, or lighting technicians, or hotel banquet managers. And with them, my panic is entirely about them judging my professionalism and my competence because of how wrong my face looks, not about whether we're going to be best friends or go on a date. It's a totally different kind of pressure. \n\nIf I think about it hypothetically... like, say my high school friends actually dragged me out to a bar or a club on a Friday night—which they don't, because they know I'm usually completely dead to the world by 9 PM if I don't have an active event running—but if they did, and some guy came up to talk to me, would I be worried he's staring at how asymmetrical I am? I mean, maybe? Probably? But I haven't actually sat around agonizing over it or feeling self-conscious about that specific scenario, because it's just not on my radar at all. \n\nI actually had a coworker ask me a few months ago—we were setting up this massive balloon arch for a corporate launch—and she asked why I wasn't on any of the dating apps, like Hinge or Bumble or whatever everyone is using right now. And I remember just laughing because, first of all, I don't have time to swipe on my phone when I'm constantly putting out fires with vendors. But even then, I wasn't thinking to myself, \"Oh, I can't make a profile because no one will match with me once they see how uneven my features are.\" I just literally don't have the mental bandwidth to even entertain the idea of dating or making new friends right now. \n\nMy brain is so completely consumed with making sure my face looks acceptable for my clients and my boss, and trying to find the perfect lighting for Zoom calls, that there is just zero space left to worry about romantic partners. It takes up so much energy just surviving my work day. So, yeah, I guess I haven't really worried about it affecting my ability to connect with someone new, just because that whole part of my life doesn't even exist right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt so distressed about how your face looks that it affected your ability to function in social situations, personal relationships, or other important areas of your life, even outside of work?\n\nPatient: I know you keep asking about things outside of work, and I... I really want to give you a different answer, I do, but it just all comes back to my job because, like I said, that literally *is* my entire life right now. But if we're talking about just... functioning? Like, my actual ability to get through a normal day and do the things I absolutely have to do? It’s... it’s honestly so bad. It really is. \n\nEven though it's not ruining a romantic relationship or, you know, a social life that I don't even have, it is completely destroying my ability to function in the one area of my life that actually matters. Like I was saying earlier, missing that planning deadline... that was just a massive, terrifying wake-up call for me. I mean, I literally sat there paralyzed. I spent so much time dealing with how I looked in those event materials, just agonizing over that one stupid headshot, that I completely blew a major deadline for a huge client. My boss had to rush the printing, and I was just sitting there feeling so incredibly guilty, but in the moment, I literally couldn't stop myself from obsessing over it. I couldn't function. I couldn't just hit send. \n\nAnd it’s not even just the big, high-pressure deadlines, either. It’s the daily functioning that's falling apart. The vendor calls and the meeting prep... they just take me so, so much longer than they should now. I used to be so incredibly efficient! I’d just pop open my laptop and talk to the caterers or the linen rental people and get it done. Now? Now I have to block out, like, almost an hour before every single video call just to deal with my appearance. I’m tweaking the ring light, I’m moving my monitor up on stacks of books, I’m trying to arrange my hair to hide the uneven side of my face... and it just eats up my entire day. \n\nSo, no, it's not really affecting a boyfriend or a hobby, but it is absolutely affecting my ability to function in the most important area of my life. I'm just constantly, constantly playing catch-up because all my time and energy goes into trying to fix how my face looks just so I can get through a basic meeting.\n\nDoctor: Even if you’re not currently in those situations, have you ever imagined or worried that something like going to a party, starting a new relationship, or meeting new people would feel impossible or extremely distressing because of how you see your face?\n\nPatient: I mean... no? Not really. I know we keep circling back to this whole idea of me having a life outside of my job, but I honestly just don't sit around picturing myself at parties or on dates, so I don't really have the chance to worry about how my face would ruin them. \n\nIt's just... like I said, my brain is so completely maxed out. If I try to force myself to imagine it right now, like, okay, let's say someone invited me to a massive New Year's Eve party. Not as the planner, but just as a normal guest. Which, just for the record, would literally never happen because NYE is our busiest season for high-end galas and I'm always running around with a clipboard and a headset until three in the morning. But if I actually *did* go, would I be stressed about my face? I mean, sure, probably? If the lighting in the venue was awful or if someone whipped out their phone to take a group photo with a flash, I'm sure I'd panic about how uneven my jaw looks and try to hide in the back. \n\nBut the thing is, I don't actually *worry* about those scenarios in my day-to-day life because there's just zero room left in my head for it. It’s like... if your house is actively on fire, you aren't sitting on the lawn worrying about whether it might rain next Tuesday, you know? My job is the fire. Making sure I don't look completely deformed in front of my clients and vendors is the fire. I just don't have the luxury of daydreaming about meeting new people, let alone agonizing over whether my face would make it impossible. \n\nIt already *is* impossible, just purely based on my schedule alone. Like, last week I worked a fourteen-hour day setting up a botanical garden wedding, and the only thing I was distressed about was whether the videographer caught my bad side while I was directing the florists. I spent like three hours the next day just feeling sick to my stomach about where I was standing in relation to his camera. That's the kind of stuff that keeps me up at night. So, yeah, I haven't really imagined or stressed over the whole relationship or party thing. It’s just totally not on my radar.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform certain behaviors or mental rituals—like checking your appearance, adjusting your posture, or replaying conversations in your head—to reduce the anxiety or discomfort you feel about how your face looks?\n\nPatient: Oh, god, yes. I mean, constantly. It’s not just a quick glance in the mirror before I leave the house, it is... it's like a whole production. Like, especially before I send event pictures to a client or right before I have to join one of those vendor meetings I was telling you about. I can't just jump into it. I will sit there and open the front-facing camera on my phone, not even to take a picture at first, just using it as a mirror to check my face, over and over again. And if I have to send a photo of a venue setup and I accidentally catch my reflection in a window or a mirror in the background? Forget it. I will retake those photos literally twenty or thirty times. \n\nAnd I have this whole routine with how I hold myself, too. Like, I’ll tilt my head a certain way—usually down and slightly to the left, because in my head, that kind of shadows the worst part of the asymmetry—but then I panic that I’m holding my neck stiffly and people are going to notice *that*, so I have to re-check it on my phone camera again to see if the tilt looks natural. It’s exhausting. \n\nSometimes I even drag my coworkers into it, which I know is terrible. Like that same girl I mentioned earlier, the one who asked me about the dating apps? We share a workspace at the venue, and I am constantly turning to her before a Zoom call or before I send out a gallery link and asking, \"Wait, does my face look weird today? Is this lighting making my jaw look worse?\" And she always just sighs and says I look fine, but I feel like I *have* to ask, just to brace myself for what the client or the vendors are going to see. I just need that tiny bit of reassurance, even if I don't really believe her.\n\nBut the worst part is just how much time it all takes. I’ll think I’m just going to do a quick two-minute check before I send an email with the event proofs, and suddenly forty-five minutes have vanished. I’m just stuck in this loop of checking my phone, adjusting my head tilt, taking a test picture, deleting it, checking again... and it eats up so much time that I end up falling way behind on my actual planning stuff. That’s exactly how I missed that deadline I told you about earlier. I get so hyper-focused on trying to fix how I look or hide how weird my face is that I completely drop the ball on confirming the caterers or the floral deliveries. It's like I physically cannot move on to my actual job until I've done all these checks, even though I know it's completely ruining my schedule.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt so upset about how your face looks that it made it hard for you to interact with others, even in small ways, like talking to someone at the grocery store or feeling comfortable in a group setting?\n\nPatient: I mean, honestly, I know we keep talking about these outside situations, like the grocery store or hanging out in groups, but it really just all comes back to work for me, because that’s where I interact with people the most. That's where it's really hitting me hard. Like, I know I already mentioned this, but it’s just the perfect example of how bad it’s gotten... I literally missed part of a major planning deadline because I was just completely paralyzed, spending so much time dealing with how I looked in those event materials. I was just staring at that headshot, zooming in, trying to figure out if my jaw looked as crooked to everyone else as it did to me, and the time just vanished. I couldn't even focus on the actual work I was supposed to be doing, which was getting those proofs approved and sent off. \n\nAnd it’s not just the big, dramatic moments like missing a deadline, either. It’s the little, everyday interactions that are just becoming so incredibly difficult. Like, I used to just pick up the phone or hop on a video call with a vendor without a second thought. But now? Now, vendor calls and meeting prep just take me so much longer than they should. I have to spend all this time adjusting my camera angle, making sure the lighting isn't hitting the uneven side of my face, trying to position myself so I don't look completely deformed when I'm talking to the florists or the caterers. I'm just so hyper-aware of how I look that I can't even be present in the conversation. I'm nodding and smiling, but in my head, I'm just panicking about whether they're staring at my face and thinking about how weird I look. \n\nSo, yeah, I guess it does make it hard to interact with people, but for me, it's just so heavily tied to my job. I'm not really out there going to grocery stores or hanging out in groups anyway, because I'm always working, but the interactions I *do* have at work are just becoming so exhausting and time-consuming because of all this. It's like I'm constantly fighting this uphill battle just to get through a basic conversation without completely losing my mind over how my face looks.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I feel like we're kind of circling the same drain here, because... well, no, I don't really avoid people. I literally can't. I have to spend all this time adjusting my camera angle, making sure the lighting isn't hitting the uneven side of my face, trying to position myself so I don't look completely deformed when I'm talking to the florists or the caterers. I'm just so hyper-aware of how I look that I can't even be present in the conversation. I'm nodding and smiling, but in my head, I'm just panicking about whether they're staring at my face and thinking about how weird I look. \n\nSo, yeah, I guess it does make it hard to interact with people, but for me, it's just so heavily tied to my job. I'm not really out there going to grocery stores or hanging out in groups anyway, because I'm always working, but the interactions I *do* have at work are just becoming so exhausting and time-consuming because of all this. It's like I'm constantly fighting this uphill battle just to get through a basic conversation without completely losing my mind over how my face looks.\n\nDoctor: Even if you aren’t currently in many social situations, have you ever felt so anxious or self-conscious about your appearance that you avoided interacting with others, or felt like you couldn’t be yourself around people, even in small everyday interactions?\n\nPatient: I feel like we're kind of circling the same drain here, because... well, no, I don't really avoid people. I don't really have the option to just tap out of interacting with people. \n\nIf I avoided talking to people, the entire event would literally collapse. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Honestly, no, not really. And I think that's why I keep bringing everything back to work, because... well, let me explain how it actually is when I finally get a minute to myself. Which, as you can probably guess with my schedule, is incredibly rare. 

But when I finally get home after a fourteen-hour day of running around a venue, dealing with the caterers, the lighting guys, the frantic bridal parties... when I finally walk into my apartment, kick off my shoes, and shut the door, it's almost like a switch flips. I mean, I'm just so physically and mentally exhausted at that point. I'll just crash on my couch, usually in my sweatpants, and I'm not... I'm not sitting there staring in the bathroom mirror or freaking out about my jawline. Because there's no one there to see it, you know? 

The whole panic, the checking my phone camera fifty times, the agonizing over the angles and the shadows... that all happens because I know *other* people are going to be looking at me. It's the anticipation of their eyes on me, of the clients and the vendors judging me, that drives me completely crazy. But if I'm just alone on a rare Sunday off, just sitting in my living room watching Netflix or doing laundry? I mean, sure, I know I still look the way I look. It's not like I magically forget that my face is asymmetrical. But it doesn't ruin my day. I'm not paralyzed by it when I'm just by myself. It doesn't stop me from, you know, folding my clothes or making dinner. 

The distress really just... it deflates when the audience is gone. I don't have to put on a show or prove that I'm this perfectly put-together event planner when it's just me in my apartment. So yeah, if I'm totally alone and I know for a fact that absolutely no one is evaluating me, it's not really this intense thing that stops me from functioning. It's just... quiet.

What brings you here today?\n\nPatient: Hi, um, thank you for seeing me. I guess I'm here because things have just gotten really, really overwhelming lately, especially with my job. I'm an event planner—mostly doing corporate meetings and conventions and stuff like that—which is a huge deal for me because I'm only 20 and I just have my high school diploma, so I really, really want to prove myself and do a good job. But recently, I've had to be a lot more front-facing, like actually out in front of people and clients, directing things. And ever since that started, I just... I can't stop looking at this one specific part of my face. \n\nIt's just so incredibly uneven. Whenever there are photos from the events—and there are always photos, you know, for the company social media, or the venue's promotional stuff, or just people tagging us—I find myself just staring at it. I will literally save the pictures to my phone and just zoom in and stare at how asymmetrical and wrong it looks. I can't stop doing it. And the frustrating part is, when I try to point it out to my coworkers or my mom or whoever, they all tell me they barely see anything. They say it's just the lighting or that they wouldn't have even noticed if I hadn't said anything, which makes no sense to me because it is so obvious. It's just right there. But I can't stop focusing on it, and it's just taking up so much of my mental energy every single time I have to be in front of a crowd or a camera, and I just don't know how to turn it off...\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your concerns about this part of your face affect you even when you're not at work or in front of people, like when you're alone at home or trying to relax?\n\nPatient: Um, I mean... I'm not really sure? Like, I don't know if I'd say it affects me in a specific way when I'm just at home, because I'm not exactly, you know, putting on a big presentation for my cat. But I guess... well, it's hard to explain. I don't really have a specific thing I do. \n\nI'll come home after a massive 12-hour shift at a hotel convention center, right? My feet are absolutely killing me, I've been wrangling caterers and A/V guys all day, and all I want to do is just sit on my couch, drink some tea, and watch something mindless on TV to decompress. But I can't really relax. It's not like I'm doing anything specific about my face when I'm alone, I just... I can't turn the thoughts off. I'll just be sitting there in my sweatpants, and my brain just loops back to how uneven I looked when I was talking to the keynote speaker earlier that afternoon. And I'll start wondering if the lighting in the main ballroom made the asymmetry look even worse than it usually does, or if they were staring at it while I was trying to explain the schedule changes to them. \n\nSo I don't really know if that answers your question, because I haven't really noticed it making me do anything differently at home, I just... the worry is just always there, taking up space in my head. Even when no one else is around to see me, I'm just sitting there overthinking every single interaction I had that day, wondering how glaringly obvious it was to everyone else.\n\nDoctor: When you're thinking about how your face looks, do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly—like checking mirrors, taking pictures, touching your face, or going over conversations in your head—to try to fix or confirm how it looks?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah... I mean, absolutely. It's actually becoming a really big problem, especially with the photos and the meetings. Like, okay, for example... before I send out any event pictures to a client, or even just before I join a regular vendor meeting—like, say I'm jumping on a Zoom call with a florist or a lighting tech to go over the floor plan—I will sit there with my phone camera open just... checking. \n\nI'll just stare at myself in the front-facing camera, trying to see how bad it is today. And if I have to take a photo of myself at the venue, or if I'm trying to send a quick update shot where I'm in the frame, I will literally retake it like twenty, thirty times. I'll tilt my head this specific way—like, if I turn just a few degrees to the left and tilt my chin down, sometimes the lighting doesn't catch the unevenness as much. But then if I move even a millimeter, it's back and it looks awful. So I'm constantly adjusting my angle, trying to find a way to hold my head where my face just looks normal, or at least passable. \n\nAnd then, even if I get an angle that's okay, I still don't trust my own eyes, so I'll go find one of my coworkers. I'll literally pull someone aside while we're setting up and be like, \"Does my face look weird today? Like, is the asymmetry super obvious right now?\" And of course they always say no, or that I look fine, but I just have to ask because I feel like I'm going crazy and I need someone to confirm it. \n\nBut the worst part is, doing all of this—the checking my phone, the retaking photos over and over, trying to fix my angle, tracking down a coworker to ask them how I look—it just eats up so much time. Like, an embarrassing amount of time. I'm supposed to be finalizing timelines and confirming headcounts for these huge corporate events, and instead I'm spending twenty minutes locked in the venue bathroom or hiding behind a pipe-and-drape setup just checking my camera. It's actually made me fall behind on my actual planning stuff a few times lately, which stresses me out even more because, like I said, I really need to prove myself at this job and I can't afford to be missing deadlines just because I'm obsessing over a photo. So, yeah, I guess I do do a lot of things like that. Probably more than I even realize, honestly, because it just feels like this exhausting routine I have to go through before I can actually do my job.\n\nDoctor: When you're focused on this part of your face, does it feel like you're mainly worried about how others are perceiving your appearance, or is it more that you feel intensely bothered by how it looks to you, regardless of what others might think?\n\nPatient: Oh, wow. Probably more than I even realize, honestly, because it just feels like this exhausting routine I have to go through before I can actually do my job.\n\nDoctor: When you're focused on this part of your face, does it feel like you're mainly worried about how others are perceiving your appearance, or is it more that you feel intensely bothered by how it looks to you, regardless of what others might think?\n\nPatient: Oh, wow. Um... I don't know. I honestly don't think I can even tell the difference anymore? Like, I really don't have a clear answer for that, because it just feels like this massive, tangled web in my head and I can't separate the two things. \n\nLet me try to explain. So, if I'm in the venue bathroom—like last weekend, we were doing this huge three-day tech summit at the Marriott, and I was hiding in the restroom checking my face in that awful, harsh fluorescent lighting they always have. I'm staring at the unevenness, and I feel almost physically sick about how wrong it looks to *me*. Like, my own eyes are seeing it, and my brain is just screaming that it's completely asymmetrical and hideous. So in that exact second, it feels like it's just me, right? It’s me being intensely bothered by my own reflection. \n\nBut then... the whole reason I'm in the bathroom checking in the first place is because I know I have to walk back out into the main ballroom and hand a wireless mic to the keynote speaker. And the entire time I'm walking down that hallway, all I'm thinking is, \"Oh my god, everyone at table four is going to see the asymmetry when I turn my head to adjust the mic stand.\" So then it's entirely about them and what they're seeing. \n\nI've actually thought about this before, like, if I lived on a deserted island, would I still care? And I mean, probably? Because I'd still know it's there. But at the same time, my entire career is so heavily based on optics. Event planning is literally all about making things look perfect for other people. You know, if the floral centerpieces are even slightly off-center, the client is going to notice and complain. So my brain just tells me that if my face is off-center, of course they're noticing that too. \n\nSo when you ask if it's my own perception or my worry about other people... I just really couldn't tell you. I don't know where my own opinion ends and my fear of their opinion begins, if that makes any sense at all? It all just blends together into this one giant, exhausting panic that follows me around all day. I wish I could give you a straight answer, but I just really don't know.\n\nDoctor: Have these concerns about your face ever caused you to avoid certain situations, like public events or even work-related tasks, because you were afraid of how your face might look or how others might react to it?\n\nPatient: Oh my gosh, yes. It’s... honestly, it’s getting to the point where it’s really messing with my actual job, which is terrifying because I love what I do and I'm only twenty, so I really need to prove I can handle this industry. But, like, just a couple of weeks ago, I actually missed part of a major planning deadline, and it was entirely because of this. We were putting together these digital welcome packets and event materials for a huge corporate retreat—like, a three-day thing with team-building exercises and gala dinners, the whole nine yards. And part of the packet was this \"Meet Your Event Team\" page, which meant I had to submit a headshot. \n\nI mean, I completely spiraled. I must have spent hours—literally hours—just trying to get a picture where the unevenness in my face didn't look completely glaring. I was taking photos in my bathroom, then by the window, then in the hallway, trying to find lighting that would just hide it. And then I kept putting off sending the file to the graphic designer because I just couldn't bear the thought of all those corporate executives staring at my asymmetrical face in their welcome brochures. I kept thinking, \"I can't send this, they'll think I look awful.\" So I just kept delaying it, and tweaking it, and obsessing over it, to the point where I completely missed the deadline to get the materials to the printer on time. My boss had to rush-order it, and I had to make up some excuse about my laptop freezing because I was too embarrassed to say, \"Hey, I couldn't send the email because I spent four hours agonizing over my face.\"\n\nAnd it's not just the big stuff like that, either. It’s bleeding into the everyday things. Like, vendor calls and meeting prep take me so, so much longer than they should now. It used to be that if I had a Zoom call with a florist or a rental company to go over a floor plan, I'd just open my laptop and join the meeting. Now? I need at least thirty to forty-five minutes of prep time just for a basic check-in call. I have to set up my ring light exactly right, I have to position my camera at this very specific high angle, and I spend forever adjusting my hair to try and cover the side of my face that looks worse. If the lighting changes because a cloud goes over the sun outside, I panic and have to turn my whole desk around. \n\nSo, yeah, I try to avoid having my camera on whenever I can get away with it, but in this business, people want to see your face, they want that personal connection. So when I have to do it, the amount of time I waste just trying to make sure I don't look horrifying is... it's exhausting. It just drags everything out, and I'm constantly working late now just to catch up on the actual planning work I should have been doing while I was busy fussing over my camera angle. So, yes, it definitely makes me avoid certain things, and when I can't avoid them, it just takes over completely.\n\nDoctor: Have these concerns about your face ever affected areas of your life outside of work, like your personal relationships, hobbies, or social activities?\n\nPatient: Um... honestly? Not really? Or, I mean, I guess I'm not entirely sure because... well, my whole life right now is basically just work. \n\nLike, I'm only twenty, and getting into event planning right out of high school means I have to hustle twice as hard as everyone else who actually went to college for hospitality or communications or whatever. So, as far as hobbies or social activities go... I don't really have any right now. My typical week is like, sixty to seventy hours of vendor meetings, site visits, drafting proposals, and answering client emails at two in the morning. I don't really go out with friends much anymore because by the time the weekend rolls around, I'm usually working a wedding or running a corporate retreat, or if I actually have a day off, I'm just completely dead on my couch catching up on sleep.\n\nSo I haven't really had the chance to see if this whole face thing ruins my personal life, because I kind of just... don't have one to ruin? Like, I'm not dating anyone right now, so I don't have to worry about a boyfriend looking at my face in weird lighting or waking up next to someone and panicking about the angle they're seeing me from. And my friends from high school, we mostly just text or send each other TikToks in our group chat, so I don't have to sit across from them at a restaurant and wonder if they're staring at my asymmetry over brunch. \n\nI mean, I think about it sometimes. Like, maybe if I did have time to go to parties or join a gym or, I don't know, take a pottery class or something normal people do, I'd probably be in the bathroom there checking my camera too. I can totally picture myself doing that. But since my entire existence is just this job, all that panic and obsession is just totally concentrated on my work stuff. Which is almost worse, honestly, when you think about it, because work is the one thing I really, really can't mess up. If I screw up a hobby, who cares, right? Like, oh well, I'm bad at pottery. But if I screw up a client's 50th-anniversary gala because I was hiding behind a curtain analyzing my chin on my iPhone, that's my whole career on the line. \n\nSo... yeah. I guess outside of work, it's not really a thing I've noticed causing problems. But only because work is literally my entire universe right now, so there's nowhere else for it to even show up, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Even though you're very focused on work, have you ever felt so self-conscious about your face that you worried it would affect your ability to connect with someone new, like a potential friend or romantic partner, if you had the chance?\n\nPatient: Um... I don't think so? Like, honestly, no, I haven't really had that specific fear, to be completely honest. Because, well, to even worry about whether my face is going to ruin a connection with a potential friend or a romantic partner, I feel like I'd have to actually be in a position where I'm trying to meet people like that, right? And I'm just... not. \n\nI mean, sure, I meet new people literally every single day, but they're always, you know, caterers, or lighting technicians, or hotel banquet managers. And with them, my panic is entirely about them judging my professionalism and my competence because of how wrong my face looks, not about whether we're going to be best friends or go on a date. It's a totally different kind of pressure. \n\nIf I think about it hypothetically... like, say my high school friends actually dragged me out to a bar or a club on a Friday night—which they don't, because they know I'm usually completely dead to the world by 9 PM if I don't have an active event running—but if they did, and some guy came up to talk to me, would I be worried he's staring at how asymmetrical I am? I mean, maybe? Probably? But I haven't actually sat around agonizing over it or feeling self-conscious about that specific scenario, because it's just not on my radar at all. \n\nI actually had a coworker ask me a few months ago—we were setting up this massive balloon arch for a corporate launch—and she asked why I wasn't on any of the dating apps, like Hinge or Bumble or whatever everyone is using right now. And I remember just laughing because, first of all, I don't have time to swipe on my phone when I'm constantly putting out fires with vendors. But even then, I wasn't thinking to myself, \"Oh, I can't make a profile because no one will match with me once they see how uneven my features are.\" I just literally don't have the mental bandwidth to even entertain the idea of dating or making new friends right now. \n\nMy brain is so completely consumed with making sure my face looks acceptable for my clients and my boss, and trying to find the perfect lighting for Zoom calls, that there is just zero space left to worry about romantic partners. It takes up so much energy just surviving my work day. So, yeah, I guess I haven't really worried about it affecting my ability to connect with someone new, just because that whole part of my life doesn't even exist right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt so distressed about how your face looks that it affected your ability to function in social situations, personal relationships, or other important areas of your life, even outside of work?\n\nPatient: I know you keep asking about things outside of work, and I... I really want to give you a different answer, I do, but it just all comes back to my job because, like I said, that literally *is* my entire life right now. But if we're talking about just... functioning? Like, my actual ability to get through a normal day and do the things I absolutely have to do? It’s... it’s honestly so bad. It really is. \n\nEven though it's not ruining a romantic relationship or, you know, a social life that I don't even have, it is completely destroying my ability to function in the one area of my life that actually matters. Like I was saying earlier, missing that planning deadline... that was just a massive, terrifying wake-up call for me. I mean, I literally sat there paralyzed. I spent so much time dealing with how I looked in those event materials, just agonizing over that one stupid headshot, that I completely blew a major deadline for a huge client. My boss had to rush the printing, and I was just sitting there feeling so incredibly guilty, but in the moment, I literally couldn't stop myself from obsessing over it. I couldn't function. I couldn't just hit send. \n\nAnd it’s not even just the big, high-pressure deadlines, either. It’s the daily functioning that's falling apart. The vendor calls and the meeting prep... they just take me so, so much longer than they should now. I used to be so incredibly efficient! I’d just pop open my laptop and talk to the caterers or the linen rental people and get it done. Now? Now I have to block out, like, almost an hour before every single video call just to deal with my appearance. I’m tweaking the ring light, I’m moving my monitor up on stacks of books, I’m trying to arrange my hair to hide the uneven side of my face... and it just eats up my entire day. \n\nSo, no, it's not really affecting a boyfriend or a hobby, but it is absolutely affecting my ability to function in the most important area of my life. I'm just constantly, constantly playing catch-up because all my time and energy goes into trying to fix how my face looks just so I can get through a basic meeting.\n\nDoctor: Even if you’re not currently in those situations, have you ever imagined or worried that something like going to a party, starting a new relationship, or meeting new people would feel impossible or extremely distressing because of how you see your face?\n\nPatient: I mean... no? Not really. I know we keep circling back to this whole idea of me having a life outside of my job, but I honestly just don't sit around picturing myself at parties or on dates, so I don't really have the chance to worry about how my face would ruin them. \n\nIt's just... like I said, my brain is so completely maxed out. Which, just for the record, would literally never happen because NYE is our busiest season for high-end galas and I'm always running around with a clipboard and a headset until three in the morning. But if I actually *did* go, would I be stressed about my face? I mean, sure, probably? If the lighting in the venue was awful or if someone whipped out their phone to take a group photo with a flash, I'm sure I'd panic about how uneven my jaw looks and try to hide in the back. \n\nBut the thing is, I don't actually *worry* about those scenarios in my day-to-day life because there's just zero room left in my head for it. Making sure I don't look completely deformed in front of my clients and vendors is the fire. I just don't have the luxury of daydreaming about meeting new people, let alone agonizing over whether my face would make it impossible. \n\nIt already *is* impossible, just purely based on my schedule alone. Like, last week I worked a fourteen-hour day setting up a botanical garden wedding, and the only thing I was distressed about was whether the videographer caught my bad side while I was directing the florists. I spent like three hours the next day just feeling sick to my stomach about where I was standing in relation to his camera. That's the kind of stuff that keeps me up at night. So, yeah, I haven't really imagined or stressed over the whole relationship or party thing. It’s just totally not on my radar.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform certain behaviors or mental rituals—like checking your appearance, adjusting your posture, or replaying conversations in your head—to reduce the anxiety or discomfort you feel about how your face looks?\n\nPatient: Oh, god, yes. I mean, constantly. It’s not just a quick glance in the mirror before I leave the house, it is... it's like a whole production. Like, especially before I send event pictures to a client or right before I have to join one of those vendor meetings I was telling you about. I can't just jump into it. I will sit there and open the front-facing camera on my phone, not even to take a picture at first, just using it as a mirror to check my face, over and over again. And if I have to send a photo of a venue setup and I accidentally catch my reflection in a window or a mirror in the background? Forget it. I will retake those photos literally twenty or thirty times. \n\nAnd I have this whole routine with how I hold myself, too. Like, I’ll tilt my head a certain way—usually down and slightly to the left, because in my head, that kind of shadows the worst part of the asymmetry—but then I panic that I’m holding my neck stiffly and people are going to notice *that*, so I have to re-check it on my phone camera again to see if the tilt looks natural. It’s exhausting. \n\nSometimes I even drag my coworkers into it, which I know is terrible. Like that same girl I mentioned earlier, the one who asked me about the dating apps? We share a workspace at the venue, and I am constantly turning to her before a Zoom call or before I send out a gallery link and asking, \"Wait, does my face look weird today? Is this lighting making my jaw look worse?\" And she always just sighs and says I look fine, but I feel like I *have* to ask, just to brace myself for what the client or the vendors are going to see. I just need that tiny bit of reassurance, even if I don't really believe her.\n\nBut the worst part is just how much time it all takes. I’ll think I’m just going to do a quick two-minute check before I send an email with the event proofs, and suddenly forty-five minutes have vanished. I’m just stuck in this loop of checking my phone, adjusting my head tilt, taking a test picture, deleting it, checking again... and it eats up so much time that I end up falling way behind on my actual planning stuff. That’s exactly how I missed that deadline I told you about earlier. I get so hyper-focused on trying to fix how I look or hide how weird my face is that I completely drop the ball on confirming the caterers or the floral deliveries. It's like I physically cannot move on to my actual job until I've done all these checks, even though I know it's completely ruining my schedule.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt so upset about how your face looks that it made it hard for you to interact with others, even in small ways, like talking to someone at the grocery store or feeling comfortable in a group setting?\n\nPatient: I mean, honestly, I know we keep talking about these outside situations, like the grocery store or hanging out in groups, but it really just all comes back to work for me, because that’s where I interact with people the most. That's where it's really hitting me hard. Like, I know I already mentioned this, but it’s just the perfect example of how bad it’s gotten... I was just staring at that headshot, zooming in, trying to figure out if my jaw looked as crooked to everyone else as it did to me, and the time just vanished. I couldn't even focus on the actual work I was supposed to be doing, which was getting those proofs approved and sent off. \n\nAnd it’s not just the big, dramatic moments like missing a deadline, either. I'm nodding and smiling, but in my head, I'm just panicking about whether they're staring at my face and thinking about how weird I look. \n\nSo, yeah, I guess it does make it hard to interact with people, but for me, it's just so heavily tied to my job. I'm not really out there going to grocery stores or hanging out in groups anyway, because I'm always working, but the interactions I *do* have at work are just becoming so exhausting and time-consuming because of all this. It's like I'm constantly fighting this uphill battle just to get through a basic conversation without completely losing my mind over how my face looks.\n\nDoctor: Even if you aren’t currently in many social situations, have you ever felt so anxious or self-conscious about your appearance that you avoided interacting with others, or felt like you couldn’t be yourself around people, even in small everyday interactions?\n\nPatient: I feel like we're kind of circling the same drain here, because... well, no, I don't really avoid people. I literally can't. It's not like I'm hiding in my apartment refusing to sign for packages or running away from the barista at the coffee shop. I don't really have the option to just tap out of interacting with people. \n\nIf I avoided talking to people, the entire event would literally collapse. Like, if a rental company delivers the wrong chiavari chairs—which happens way more often than you'd think, by the way, it is a constant nightmare—I can't just hide in the venue's coat closet because I'm having a bad day with how my face looks. I have to march right up to the delivery guys and sort it out right then and there. And as for feeling like I can't be myself... I honestly don't even know what my 'self' is outside of work anymore anyway. I'm just the girl with the headset and the master timeline. \n\nSo, no, I don't avoid the interactions. I still do them. I just do them while I'm completely stressing out about the lighting and my jawline, like I was telling you before. I'll still talk to the florist, I'll still direct the catering staff, I'll just do it while trying to casually stand behind a floral arch or angle myself toward a shadow so they can't see the bad side of my face. But I don't actually avoid the interaction itself. I push through it because I have to. The bride doesn't care if I'm feeling hideous, she just wants to know if the cocktail hour is starting on time and if the signature drinks are ready. So yeah, I don't really relate to the whole avoiding people or not being myself thing, because I'm always there, I'm just miserable while I'm doing it.\n\nDoctor: Even when you’re not interacting with others or in a situation where your appearance is being evaluated, do you still feel intense distress or preoccupation about the asymmetry in your face that significantly interferes with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no, not really. And I think that's why I keep bringing everything back to work, because... well, let me explain how it actually is when I finally get a minute to myself. Which, as you can probably guess with my schedule, is incredibly rare. \n\nBut when I finally get home after a fourteen-hour day of running around a venue, dealing with the caterers, the lighting guys, the frantic bridal parties... when I finally walk into my apartment, kick off my shoes, and shut the door, it's almost like a switch flips. I mean, I'm just so physically and mentally exhausted at that point. I'll just crash on my couch, usually in my sweatpants, and I'm not... I'm not sitting there staring in the bathroom mirror or freaking out about my jawline. It's the anticipation of their eyes on me, of the clients and the vendors judging me, that drives me completely crazy. But if I'm just alone on a rare Sunday off, just sitting in my living room watching Netflix or doing laundry? I mean, sure, I know I still look the way I look. It's not like I magically forget that my face is asymmetrical. But it doesn't ruin my day. I'm not paralyzed by it when I'm just by myself. It doesn't stop me from, you know, folding my clothes or making dinner. \n\nThe distress really just... it deflates when the audience is gone. I don't have to put on a show or prove that I'm this perfectly put-together event planner when it's just me in my apartment. I couldn't just hit send. \n\nAnd it’s not even just the big, high-pressure deadlines, either. It’s the daily functioning that's falling apart. I’d just pop open my laptop and talk to the caterers or the linen rental people and get it done. Now? I’m tweaking the ring light, I’m moving my monitor up on stacks of books, I’m trying to arrange my hair to hide the uneven side of my face... and it just eats up my entire day. \n\nSo, no, it's not really affecting a boyfriend or a hobby, but it is absolutely affecting my ability to function in the most important area of my life. I'm just constantly, constantly playing catch-up because all my time and energy goes into trying to fix how my face looks just so I can get through a basic meeting.\n\nDoctor: Even if you’re not currently in those situations, have you ever imagined or worried that something like going to a party, starting a new relationship, or meeting new people would feel impossible or extremely distressing because of how you see your face?\n\nPatient: I mean... no? Not really. I really want to give you a different answer, I do, but it just all comes back to my job because, like I said, that literally *is* my entire life right now. But if we're talking about just... functioning? Like, my actual ability to get through a normal day and do the things I absolutely have to do? It’s... it’s honestly so bad. It really is. \n\nEven though it's not ruining a romantic relationship or, you know, a social life that I don't even have, it is completely destroying my ability to function in the one area of my life that actually matters. Like I was saying earlier, missing that planning deadline... that was just a massive, terrifying wake-up call for me. I mean, I literally sat there paralyzed. I spent so much time dealing with how I looked in those event materials, just agonizing over that one stupid headshot, that I completely blew a major deadline for a huge client. My boss had to rush the printing, and I was just sitting there feeling so incredibly guilty, but in the moment, I literally couldn't stop myself from obsessing over it. I couldn't function. I couldn't just hit send. \n\nAnd it’s not even just the big, high-pressure deadlines, either. It’s the daily functioning that's falling apart. The vendor calls and the meeting prep... they just take me so, so much longer than they should now. I used to be so incredibly efficient! I’d just pop open my laptop and talk to the caterers or the linen rental people and get it done. Now? Now I have to block out, like, almost an hour before every single video call just to deal with my appearance. I’m tweaking the ring light, I’m moving my monitor up on stacks of books, I’m trying to arrange my hair to hide the uneven side of my face... and it just eats up my entire day. \n\nSo, no, it's not really affecting a boyfriend or a hobby, but it is absolutely affecting my ability to function in the most important area of my life. I'm just constantly, constantly playing catch-up because all my time and energy goes into trying to fix how my face looks just so I can get through a basic meeting.\n\nDoctor: Even if you’re not currently in those situations, have you ever imagined or worried that something like going to a party, starting a new relationship, or meeting new people would feel impossible or extremely distressing because of how you see your face?\n\nPatient: I mean... no? Not really. I know we keep circling back to this whole idea of me having a life outside of my job, but I honestly just don't sit around picturing myself at parties or on dates, so I don't really have the chance to worry about how my face would ruin them. \n\nIt's just... like I said, my brain is so completely maxed out. If I try to force myself to imagine it right now, like, okay, let's say someone invited me to a massive New Year's Eve party. Not as the planner, but just as a normal guest. Which, just for the record, would literally never happen because NYE is our busiest season for high-end galas and I'm always running around with a clipboard and a headset until three in the morning. But if I actually *did* go, would I be stressed about my face? I mean, sure, probably? If the lighting in the venue was awful or if someone whipped out their phone to take a group photo with a flash, I'm sure I'd panic about how uneven my jaw looks and try to hide in the back. \n\nBut the thing is, I don't actually *worry* about those scenarios in my day-to-day life because there's just zero room left in my head for it. It’s like... if your house is actively on fire, you aren't sitting on the lawn worrying about whether it might rain next Tuesday, you know? My job is the fire. Making sure I don't look completely deformed in front of my clients and vendors is the fire. I just don't have the luxury of daydreaming about meeting new people, let alone agonizing over whether my face would make it impossible. \n\nIt already *is* impossible, just purely based on my schedule alone. Like, last week I worked a fourteen-hour day setting up a botanical garden wedding, and the only thing I was distressed about was whether the videographer caught my bad side while I was directing the florists. I spent like three hours the next day just feeling sick to my stomach about where I was standing in relation to his camera. That's the kind of stuff that keeps me up at night. So, yeah, I haven't really imagined or stressed over the whole relationship or party thing. It’s just totally not on my radar.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform certain behaviors or mental rituals—like checking your appearance, adjusting your posture, or replaying conversations in your head—to reduce the anxiety or discomfort you feel about how your face looks?\n\nPatient: Oh, god, yes. I mean, constantly. It’s not just a quick glance in the mirror before I leave the house, it is... it's like a whole production. Like, especially before I send event pictures to a client or right before I have to join one of those vendor meetings I was telling you about. I can't just jump into it. I will sit there and open the front-facing camera on my phone, not even to take a picture at first, just using it as a mirror to check my face, over and over again. And if I have to send a photo of a venue setup and I accidentally catch my reflection in a window or a mirror in the background? Forget it. I will retake those photos literally twenty or thirty times. \n\nAnd I have this whole routine with how I hold myself, too. Like, I’ll tilt my head a certain way—usually down and slightly to the left, because in my head, that kind of shadows the worst part of the asymmetry—but then I panic that I’m holding my neck stiffly and people are going to notice *that*, so I have to re-check it on my phone camera again to see if the tilt looks natural. It’s exhausting. \n\nSometimes I even drag my coworkers into it, which I know is terrible. Like that same girl I mentioned earlier, the one who asked me about the dating apps? We share a workspace at the venue, and I am constantly turning to her before a Zoom call or before I send out a gallery link and asking, \"Wait, does my face look weird today? Is this lighting making my jaw look worse?\" And she always just sighs and says I look fine, but I feel like I *have* to ask, just to brace myself for what the client or the vendors are going to see. I just need that tiny bit of reassurance, even if I don't really believe her.\n\nBut the worst part is just how much time it all takes. I’ll think I’m just going to do a quick two-minute check before I send an email with the event proofs, and suddenly forty-five minutes have vanished. I’m just stuck in this loop of checking my phone, adjusting my head tilt, taking a test picture, deleting it, checking again... and it eats up so much time that I end up falling way behind on my actual planning stuff. That’s exactly how I missed that deadline I told you about earlier. I get so hyper-focused on trying to fix how I look or hide how weird my face is that I completely drop the ball on confirming the caterers or the floral deliveries. It's like I physically cannot move on to my actual job until I've done all these checks, even though I know it's completely ruining my schedule.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt so upset about how your face looks that it made it hard for you to interact with others, even in small ways, like talking to someone at the grocery store or feeling comfortable in a group setting?\n\nPatient: I mean, honestly, I know we keep talking about these outside situations, like the grocery store or hanging out in groups, but it really just all comes back to work for me, because that’s where I interact with people the most. That's where it's really hitting me hard. Like, I know I already mentioned this, but it’s just the perfect example of how bad it’s gotten... I literally missed part of a major planning deadline because I was just completely paralyzed, spending so much time dealing with how I looked in those event materials. I was just staring at that headshot, zooming in, trying to figure out if my jaw looked as crooked to everyone else as it did to me, and the time just vanished. I couldn't even focus on the actual work I was supposed to be doing, which was getting those proofs approved and sent off. \n\nAnd it’s not just the big, dramatic moments like missing a deadline, either. It’s the little, everyday interactions that are just becoming so incredibly difficult. Like, I used to just pick up the phone or hop on a video call with a vendor without a second thought. But now? Now, vendor calls and meeting prep just take me so much longer than they should. I have to spend all this time adjusting my camera angle, making sure the lighting isn't hitting the uneven side of my face, trying to position myself so I don't look completely deformed when I'm talking to the florists or the caterers. I'm just so hyper-aware of how I look that I can't even be present in the conversation. I'm nodding and smiling, but in my head, I'm just panicking about whether they're staring at my face and thinking about how weird I look. \n\nSo, yeah, I guess it does make it hard to interact with people, but for me, it's just so heavily tied to my job. I'm not really out there going to grocery stores or hanging out in groups anyway, because I'm always working, but the interactions I *do* have at work are just becoming so exhausting and time-consuming because of all this. It's like I'm constantly fighting this uphill battle just to get through a basic conversation without completely losing my mind over how my face looks.\n\nDoctor: Even if you aren’t currently in many social situations, have you ever felt so anxious or self-conscious about your appearance that you avoided interacting with others, or felt like you couldn’t be yourself around people, even in small everyday interactions?\n\nPatient: I feel like we're kind of circling the same drain here, because... well, no, I don't really avoid people. I literally can't. It's not like I'm hiding in my apartment refusing to sign for packages or running away from the barista at the coffee shop. I don't really have the option to just tap out of interacting with people. \n\nIf I avoided talking to people, the entire event would literally collapse. Like, if a rental company delivers the wrong chiavari chairs—which happens way more often than you'd think, by the way, it is a constant nightmare—I can't just hide in the venue's coat closet because I'm having a bad day with how my face looks. I have to march right up to the delivery guys and sort it out right then and there. And as for feeling like I can't be myself... I honestly don't even know what my 'self' is outside of work anymore anyway. I'm just the girl with the headset and the master timeline. \n\nSo, no, I don't avoid the interactions. I still do them. I just do them while I'm completely stressing out about the lighting and my jawline, like I was telling you before. I'll still talk to the florist, I'll still direct the catering staff, I'll just do it while trying to casually stand behind a floral arch or angle myself toward a shadow so they can't see the bad side of my face. But I don't actually avoid the interaction itself. I push through it because I have to. The bride doesn't care if I'm feeling hideous, she just wants to know if the cocktail hour is starting on time and if the signature drinks are ready. So yeah, I don't really relate to the whole avoiding people or not being myself thing, because I'm always there, I'm just miserable while I'm doing it.\n\nDoctor: Even when you’re not interacting with others or in a situation where your appearance is being evaluated, do you still feel intense distress or preoccupation about the asymmetry in your face that significantly interferes with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no, not really. And I think that's why I keep bringing everything back to work, because... well, let me explain how it actually is when I finally get a minute to myself. Which, as you can probably guess with my schedule, is incredibly rare. \n\nBut when I finally get home after a fourteen-hour day of running around a venue, dealing with the caterers, the lighting guys, the frantic bridal parties... when I finally walk into my apartment, kick off my shoes, and shut the door, it's almost like a switch flips. I mean, I'm just so physically and mentally exhausted at that point. I'll just crash on my couch, usually in my sweatpants, and I'm not... I'm not sitting there staring in the bathroom mirror or freaking out about my jawline. Because there's no one there to see it, you know? \n\nThe whole panic, the checking my phone camera fifty times, the agonizing over the angles and the shadows... that all happens because I know *other* people are going to be looking at me. It's the anticipation of their eyes on me, of the clients and the vendors judging me, that drives me completely crazy. But if I'm just alone on a rare Sunday off, just sitting in my living room watching Netflix or doing laundry? I mean, sure, I know I still look the way I look. It's not like I magically forget that my face is asymmetrical. But it doesn't ruin my day. I'm not paralyzed by it when I'm just by myself. It doesn't stop me from, you know, folding my clothes or making dinner. \n\nThe distress really just... it deflates when the audience is gone. I don't have to put on a show or prove that I'm this perfectly put-together event planner when it's just me in my apartment. So yeah, if I'm totally alone and I know for a fact that absolutely no one is evaluating me, it's not really this intense thing that stops me from functioning. It's just... quiet.\n\nDoctor: Even when you're not at work, have you ever felt significant distress or found it difficult to function in other important areas of your life because of how you see your face?\n\nPatient: I mean, I know you’re really trying to get me to talk about other parts of my life, but honestly, I don't really *have* other important areas right now. I'm twenty years old, I went straight from high school into this crazy industry, and event planning is basically my entire existence. I don't really have time for hobbies or dating or going out. So when you ask if it makes it hard to function... I kind of have to bring it right back to what I was saying earlier, because work *is* my life. \n\nI know I already told you about this, but I really need you to understand how bad it is that I missed part of that planning deadline. In my industry, missing a deadline is basically the ultimate cardinal sin. Everything is on a master timeline, and if one thing gets delayed, the whole event schedule gets pushed back. And I literally blew past that deadline just because I was so completely consumed with how I looked in those event materials. I was just sitting there at my desk, completely paralyzed, staring at my face and agonizing over my jaw, and the time just completely evaporated. \n\nAnd it's not just a one-time thing, either. It's happening every single day. Like I mentioned before with the vendor calls and the meeting prep... it's just taking me so much longer than it ever should. A quick ten-minute video check-in with a caterer turns into this whole exhausting, drawn-out ordeal because I’m spending twenty minutes beforehand just trying to fix my camera angle and my lighting so my face doesn't look completely warped. I'm wasting so much time just trying to manage how people see me. So, I don't really know about other areas of my life, because I don't really have any, but in the one area that actually matters to me, it is absolutely making it incredibly difficult to function. It's just draining all my time and energy.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Wait, didn't we... I feel like we literally just talked about this, right? Because, like I was saying just a few minutes ago, no. I really don't.
